Table 1:
Eigenvalue assignment for open channel operators in the tricritical Ising CFT.

To determine whether an operator is neutral under
W
we compare its eigenvalue with that of the identity, which is
\zeta
is our choice of normalizations. We see that there are two neutral relevant operators, both of them are boundary changing operators living in
\mathbb{H}_{\varepsilon\sigma}
and
\mathbb{H}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}\sigma}
and have weight
h=7/16
. It is therefore possible that an appropriate combination of these deformations preserves boundary integrability and provides a parametrization of the family of solutions we have found.

7
Lattice Impurities

We now turn our attention to a different set of theories, namely quantum lattice systems defined on a chain. Indeed, these are a natural arena to study the phenomenon of categorical scattering beyond QFT, as recently noticed in
[
92
]
. The aim of this Section is first to show that (one of) the defects presented in
[
92
]
is actually symmetry reflecting and enjoys a defect anomaly, thus fitting in our general discussion, and then we construct generalizations of this model where physics is expected to be richer.

We consider two quantum many–body systems
\mathcal{T}_{L}
and
\mathcal{T}_{R}
with Hamiltonians

H_{L}=\sum_{j}h_{L,j}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptH_{R}=\sum_{j}h_{R,j}\,,

(7.1)

where
h_{L,j}
and
h_{R,j}
are quasilocal terms.
In the presence of an impurity at site
j=m
the total Hamiltonian is modified to

H_{\calD}=\sum_{j}^{m-1}h_{L,j}+\sum_{k=m+1}h_{R,k}+H_{I}\equiv H_{L}(j<m)+H_{R}(j>m)+H_{I}\,,

(7.2)

where
H_{I}
is a quasilocal Hamiltonian localized at the impurity location
j=m
. The Hilbert space at the impurity can in principle be different from the one in either spin chain, notably by the addition of further degrees of freedom.

Our construction of symmetric (and symmetry reflecting) defects is very transparent in a lattice setting. Let us assume that the two systems
\mathcal{T}_{L,R}
are symmetric with respect to two on–site symmetries generated by unitary operators
U_{L,R}
, respectively. As the Hamiltonian is made up of quasi-local pieces, the original symmetries
U_{L}
,
U_{R}
still commute with it far from the impurity:

U_{L}h_{L,j}U^{-1}_{L}=h_{L,j}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptU_{R}h_{R,j}U^{-1}_{R}=h_{R,j}\,.

(7.3)

However, at the impurity, the definition of the defect
U=U_{L}(j<m)\otimes U_{R}(j>m)
must be improved by a local unitary
u_{\calD}
localized at
j=m
:

\widetilde{U}=U_{L}(j<m)\otimes u_{\calD}\otimes U_{R}(j>m)\,,

(7.4)

to ensure commutativity with the full Hamiltonian.
Interestingly, the resulting symmetry operators might now fail to commute at the location of the interface: this signals the defect anomaly.
The study of symmetry defects on quantum chains, especially in the presence of boundaries, has been subject of several works
[
25
,
81
,
80
,
12
,
70
,
52
,
43
,
7
]
.
In the case of a symmetry-reflecting defect, we simply end the symmetry defect at the impurity site by choosing an adequate decoration.

The purpose of this section is to show in practice how this leads to a vast landscape of impurities carrying defect anomalies, which can lead to transmission of non-local particles from incident charged wavepackets according to our arguments. Furthermore, we will see that in the XYZ model we can construct in this manner nontrivial interfaces which are not connected to topological (transmissive) ones by deformations localized on the defect.

7.1
Order/Disorder Interface in Ising

A paradigmatic example is provided by the Ising model, whose interfaces have been classified in
[
69
]
. We will focus on an interface between the ordered and disordered descriptions of the Ising model
[
92
]
. This is also the lattice regularization of the model presented in Section
6
. Recall that the Ising Hamiltonian:

H_{\text{Ising}}=-\sum_{j}X_{j}X_{j+1}-h\sum_{j}Z_{j}\,,

(7.5)

flows to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
ordered/disordered phase for
h>1
and
h<1
, respectively. In (
7.5
),
X
and
Z
denote respectively the X and Z Pauli matrices acting on the two-dimensional Hilbert space localized at site
j
.
Following
[
92
]
, we consider the following interface at site
j=m
:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=H_{L}+H_{R}+H_{I}\,,

(7.6)

\displaystyle H_{L}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j}^{m-2}X_{j}X_{j+1}-h_{L}\sum_{j}^{m-1}Z_{j}\,,

\displaystyle H_{R}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=m+1}X_{j}-h_{R}\sum_{j=m+1}Z_{j}Z_{j+1}\,,

\displaystyle H_{I}

\displaystyle=-X_{m-1}X_{m}-\gamma Z_{m}Z_{m+1}\,.

For
h_{L}=h_{R}=\gamma
the interface is topological, while, for
h_{L}=h_{R}=h
and
\gamma\neq h
it describes the
\epsilon
deformation of the KW interface between ordered and disordered Ising theories we have previously analyzed. The mass of the single particle state is:

M=2\sqrt{h^{2}-1}\,,

(7.7)

so choosing different
h_{L}
and
h_{R}
can be understood as different magnitudes of
bulk

\epsilon
perturbations in the Ising CFT.

Let us look at the symmetry of this system. The left and right Hamiltonians are invariant under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetries

U_{L}=\prod_{j}Z_{j}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptU_{R}=\prod_{j}X_{j}\,,

(7.8)

respectively. The interface is symmetry reflecting under
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, since the truncated operators

\widetilde{U}_{L}=\prod_{j}^{m}Z_{j}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{U}_{R}=\prod_{j=m}X_{j}\,,

(7.9)

both commute with the defect Hamiltonian:

[\widetilde{U}_{L},H]=[\widetilde{U}_{R},H]=0\,,

(7.10)

for all values of
h_{L},h_{R},\gamma
. However the two symmetries have a defect anomaly, as they fail to commute on site
j=m
due to the presence of the defect:

\widetilde{U}_{L}\times\widetilde{U}_{R}=-\widetilde{U}_{R}\times\widetilde{U}_{L}\,.

(7.11)

From this it is clear that this type of impurity allows for transmission between local charged particles (
\sigma_{j}=X_{j}
) and twist defects (
\mu_{j}=\prod_{k=m}^{j}X_{k}
). This was explicitly shown in
[
92
]
.

7.2
Interfaces in the XYZ Model

A much richer playground for this type of defects can be found in the XYZ model, defined by the Hamiltonian

H_{XYZ}=-\sum_{j}J_{X}X_{j}X_{j+1}+J_{Y}Y_{j}Y_{j+1}+J_{Z}Z_{j}Z_{j+1}\,.

(7.12)

In this case we look for defects, i.e. localized modifications of
H_{XYZ}
. In particular, we would like to construct symmetry-reflecting impurities for the following two symmetries:

\widetilde{U}_{L}=\prod_{j}^{m-1}(-)^{j}Z_{j}\,X_{m}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{U}_{R}=Y_{m}\prod_{j=m+1}(-)^{j}Z_{j}\,,

(7.13)

generating a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{L}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{R}
symmetry. Again, an impurity commuting with
\widetilde{U}_{L,R}
will have the same
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defect anomaly following from the lack of commutativity of these two operators at site
j=m
. There are several other choices for these symmetries, which can be mapped to the above by a change of Pauli basis. Notice that:

\widetilde{U}_{L}\widetilde{U}_{R}=i\prod_{j}(-)^{j}Z_{j}\,,

(7.14)

is an order 4 operator, as implied by the defect anomaly.

To find a suitable defect Hamiltonian we first remove the kinetic terms which cross the site
m
. Apart from the obvious boundary terms
Z_{m-1}
and
Z_{m+1}
there are several interesting quadratic terms
29
29

29

We only report here the ones which are bi-linear in fermions after the Jordan-Wigner transformation.

Y_{m-1}Y_{m}\,,\ X_{m}X_{m+1}\,,\ X_{m-1}Y_{m}\,,\ X_{m}Y_{m+1}

(7.15)

and cubic terms:

X_{m-1}Z_{m}X_{m+1}\,,\ X_{m-1}Z_{m}Y_{m+1}\,,\ Y_{m-1}Z_{m}X_{m+1}\,,\ Y_{m-1}Z_{m}Y_{m+1}\,,

(7.16)

which we are free to add to the defect Hamiltonian.
To prune this further, let us impose time reversal and parity symmetry on the defect, too. In the bulk, time reversal
\mathcal{T}
acts by complex conjugation

T\;:\hskip 10.00002ptX_{j}\to X_{j}\,,\ Y_{j}\to-Y_{j}\,,Z_{j}\to Z_{j}\hskip 10.00002pt,j\not=m\,,

(7.17)

but on the defect, in order to commute with both
\widetilde{U}_{L}
and
\widetilde{U}_{R}
we must conjugate by
X_{m}
, thus

T\;:\hskip 10.00002ptX_{m}\to X_{m}\,,\ Y_{m}\to Y_{m}\,,\ Z_{m}\to-Z_{m}\,.

(7.18)

Similarly, parity exchanges sites
j\leftrightarrow 2m-j
while acting by
X_{m}\leftrightarrow Y_{m}
and
Z_{m}\to-Z_{m}
on the defect’s site. In this way, there are no anomalies with the two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetries. With this, the defect Hamiltonian takes the form:

H_{I}=\gamma\left(X_{m-1}Y_{m}+X_{m}X_{m+1}\right)+\delta\left(Y_{m-1}Z_{m}X_{m+1}-X_{m-1}Z_{m}Y_{m+1}\right)\,.

(7.19)

This defines a two-parameter family of defects
\calD(\gamma,\delta)
. Notice that, for
\gamma=0
, we can diagonalize
Z_{m}
, leading to a direct sum of two defects:

\calD(0,\delta)=\calD_{\uparrow}(\delta)\oplus\calD_{\downarrow}(-\delta)\,.

(7.20)

We now make use of the Jordan-Wigner transformation to make the physics more transparent.
On a single site we have a complex fermion
c_{j}=a_{j}+ib_{j}
and the Jordan Wigner map reads

a_{j}=\begin{cases}-X_{j}\,\prod_{k<j}(-)^{k}Z_{k}\quad&j\,\text{even}\\
Y_{j}\,\prod_{k<j}(-)^{k}Z_{k}\quad&j\,\text{odd}\end{cases}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptb_{j}=\begin{cases}Y_{j}\,\prod_{k<j}(-)^{k}Z_{k}\quad&j\,\text{even}\\
X_{j}\,\prod_{k<j}(-)^{k}Z_{k}\quad&j\,\text{odd}\end{cases}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptia_{j}b_{j}=Z_{j}\,.

(7.21)

In the fermionic variables the defect Hamiltonian is quadratic and reads (for simplicity, we take
m
to be odd):

H_{I}=H_{\gamma}+H_{\delta}=-i\gamma\left(b_{m-1}a_{m}+a_{m}a_{m+1}\right)-i\delta\left(a_{m-1}a_{m+1}+b_{m-1}b_{m+1}\right)\,

(7.22)

Notice that
b_{m}
does not appear in the Hamiltonian. This reflects the fact that the defect ground state is doubly degenerate and spanned by:

|\Omega\rangle\,,\hskip 10.00002ptb_{m}|\Omega\rangle\,.

(7.23)

In the fermionic picture this is the Majorana zero mode imposed by the Arf defect anomaly.
Now let us understand these defects in two different known regimes.

XX model.

The first model we analyze is the
XX
model, obtained by setting
J_{X}=J_{Y}=J\,,\ J_{Z}=0
and which flows to a critical
c=1
boson at
R=\sqrt{2}
(i.e. a free Dirac fermion).
30
30

30

In our conventions, the self-dual radius is
R=1
.

The theory has a KW duality symmetry
[
90
]
(see also
[
70
]
for a derivation of this symmetry on the lattice) corresponding to the gauging of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}
shift symmetry. However, the KW duality defect is not time reversal invariant on the lattice (
\calD_{KW}^{\dagger}\neq\calD_{KW}
), so we cannot find it as a limit of
H_{\calD}
. However, the corresponding continuum topological defect will appear in the long distance limit of our analysis.

The model is dual to free fermions
c_{j}=a_{j}+ib_{j}
, with the Hamiltonian:

H_{XX}^{F}=-iJ\sum_{j}a_{j}a_{j+1}+b_{j}b_{j+1}\,.

(7.24)

We analyze first the limit in which
H_{\calD}
is a topological defect. This is
(\gamma,\delta)=(0,J)
, which corresponds to a defect
\mathscr{T}
implementing a unit lattice translation, and can be understood by applying the lattice translation on sites
j\geq m
only. Around this point, we can use the expansion of the lattice field in low-energy modes, which reads:

c_{j}=\begin{cases}\psi_{L}(x)+(-)^{j}\psi_{R}(x)\,,\ \ \ &j<m\\
\psi_{L}(x)+(-)^{j+1}\psi_{R}(x)\,,\ \ \ &j>m\\
a_{m}+ib_{m}\,,\ \ \ &j=m\,,\end{cases}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptx=aj\,,

(7.25)

where
\psi_{L}=a_{L}+ib_{L},\psi_{R}=a_{R}+ib_{R}
are the continuum Weyl fermions, the shift comes from the lattice translation and we have isolated the fermion zero modes
a_{m},b_{m}
. In these variables, the lattice translation acts as chiral fermion parity
(-)^{F_{R}}
on the right Weyl fermion
[
80
]
. This is an example of an emanant symmetry
[
25
]
. Since the Weyl fermion has two Majorana components
a_{R}
and
b_{R}
, there are indeed two separate Majorana zero modes, corresponding to the edge modes of a Kitaev phase for the two separate chiral fermion parity symmetries.
If we bosonize the system (in the continuum limit), this leads to a non-simple topological defect
CU^{m}_{\pi/2}\oplus CU^{m}_{\pi/2}
(as we remarked, the zero mode
ia_{m}b_{m}=Z_{m}
is decoupled). The direct sum decomposition remains true as long as we keep
\gamma=0
.

It is simple to see using (
7.25
) that the
\delta
deformation becomes an exactly marginal mass deformation, while the
\gamma
deformation lifts the
a_{m}
zero mode via:

H_{\gamma}=-i\gamma a_{m}\left(a_{L}-b_{L}-a_{R}-b_{R}\right)\,.

(7.26)

This is a relevant deformation, driving the defect to a new fixed point. Related deformations coupling Majorana zero modes to bulk fermions have been studied in
[
78
,
61
,
87
,
47
,
73
]
. Using the methods of
[
87
]
, one can see that the IR defect corresponds to chiral fermion parity for a single Weyl-Majorana mode only, which under bosonization is mapped to the duality defect of the
c=1
compact boson.
This defect is stable, as there are no relevant bulk operators (including
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-twisted fields) which preserve both the left and right
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action, but also has an exactly marginal mass deformation. The schematic flow is described in Figure
9
.

CU_{\pi/2}\oplus U_{\pi/2}C
\delta\int_{\calD}\epsilon
\mathcal{N}
\delta\int_{\calD}\epsilon
\gamma\int_{\calD}a_{m}\,\psi

Figure 9:
Schematic structure of the symmetry reflecting defects at the
XX
point: starting from the topological defect
\calD(0,J)=CU_{\pi/2}\oplus U_{\pi/2}C
we have a conformal manifold generated by the mass deformation
\epsilon
. Deforming by
\gamma
leads to the
\epsilon
deformation of the duality defect, instead. The conformal manifolds have the topology of a segment, due to the breaking of a phantom symmetry
[
4
]
, the end-points being reflective walls.

Notice however that the topological defect is not stable under bulk deformation: if we add a
ZZ
term in the bulk (reducing the radius of the IR scalar) the energy operator
\epsilon=V_{0,1}
becomes relevant and drives the system to the stable (pinning) configuration: the two hard walls.

Heisenberg chain

Another interesting limit is the Heisenberg chain
J_{X}=J_{Y}=J_{Z}=J
, in which the system at long distances is described by the SU(2)
1
CFT. The
SU(2)_{1}
CFT (i.e. the free boson at
R=1
) is not self-dual under gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{m}
symmetry, and as such there is no perfectly transmissive impurity to deform available.
31
31

31

The
SU(2)_{1}
has another
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, acting as the center of the chiral
SU(2)_{L}
symmetry under whose gauging the theory is self-dual. Our lattice symmetry instead is part of the vector symmetry, but not central.

However, we can consider as a starting point the direct product of two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetric boundary conditions:

\calD_{0}=|f\rrangle\llangle f|\,,

(7.27)

in free boson notation,
|f\rrangle
is a Neumann boundary condition.
This defect has no defect anomaly. To remedy this, we stack a decoupled QM describing a spin 1/2 particle
z
:

S_{\text{QM}}=i\int z^{\dagger}_{i}\dot{z}^{i}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptz^{\dagger}z=2S\,.

(7.28)

which carries a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset SO(3)
anomaly
[
60
]

\omega(A)=i\pi\int\omega_{2}(A)=i\pi\int A_{1}\cup A_{2}\,,

(7.29)

and couple the two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetries to the bulk
U_{L}
and
U_{R}
, respectively

|f\rrangle
\llangle f|
S=1/2\ \text{QM}
U_{L}
U_{R}
SU(2)_{1}
SU(2)_{1}

(7.30)

The
SU(2)
generators are
S^{a}=\frac{1}{2}z^{\dagger}\sigma^{a}z
. This recovers exactly the expression (
7.13
) for the defect symmetry. Recall that, at the critical point
SU(2)_{1}
, a bulk spin excitation
S^{a}_{j}
(
a=X,Y,Z)
decomposes in the
\Delta=1
current
J^{a}
and the
\Delta=1/2

SU(2)_{1}
spin field
n^{a}
via:

S^{a}_{j}\sim J^{a}(x)+(-)^{j}n^{a}(x)\,,\hskip 10.00002ptx=aj\,,

(7.31)

up to some non-universal coefficients. Plugging this expression in the defect interaction leads to:
32
32

32

Notice also that the other exactly marginal current deformations
J_{\perp}^{a}
are forbidden by parity symmetry.

H_{I}^{SU(2)_{1}}\sim\widetilde{\delta}\left(n^{X}_{L}S^{Y}+S^{X}n^{X}_{R}\right)+\widetilde{\gamma}S^{Z}\left(n^{X}_{L}n^{Y}_{R}-n^{Y}_{L}n^{X}_{R}\right)\,.

(7.32)

The second interaction is marginal and needs to be studied in conformal perturbation theory. In the free boson language
n^{X}=\cos(\phi)
,
n_{Y}=\sin(\phi)
, and the interaction is
S^{Z}\sin(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R})\equiv S^{Z}\mathcal{O}
.
The first term in the
\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{O}
OPE is the kinetic term
\partial\phi_{L}\partial\phi_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial\phi_{+}^{2}-\partial\phi_{-}^{2}\right)
. Thus, the perturbation changes the radius of the boson at the defect, and, with the correct sign, the perturbation can be made relevant by the BKT mechanism. The overall situation differs from the one at the
XX
point, where the second deformation is instead exactly marginal. However, also in this case,
\calD(0,\gamma)
is the direct sum of two separate defects.
These are simple to discuss, by minimizing (maximizing) the sine, they impose

\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}=\pm\pi/2\,,

(7.33)

at the defect. Thus, a charged vertex operator on the left
e^{i\phi_{L}}
becomes a charged and local vertex operator
e^{i\phi_{R}}
on the right.
To recover the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
charge conservation this must be dressed by a topological defect operator, which is precisely the zero-mode
x=Z_{m}
which exists as a consequence of (
7.20
). Indeed, the presence of the defect zero mode can be immediately verified by time evolution with the defect Hamiltonian.

The first interaction instead is strongly relevant
(\Delta=1/2)
and drives the defect to a new interacting fixed point. To solve it we take a mean field approach and diagonalize the spin variable in a fixed scalar background. The Hamiltonian
A\cdot S
, with
A=(A_{X},A_{Y}))
planar, has eigenvalues
\pm\sqrt{A_{X}^{2}+A_{Y}^{2}}
and eigenvector along the
(A_{X},A_{Y})
direction. At
R=1

n^{X}\sim\cos(\phi)
, so
(A_{X},A_{Y})=(\pm,\pm)
and the IR defect is a direct sum of 4 Dirichlet bc:

\calD=|D,0\rrangle\llangle D,0|\oplus|D,\pi\rrangle\llangle D,0|\oplus|D,0\rrangle\llangle D,\pi|\oplus|D,\pi\rrangle\llangle D,\pi|\,.

(7.34)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{L}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{R}
symmetry exchanges the four by flipping the coordinates of the eigenvector.

Appendix A
Tube to Strip in the SymTFT

In this appendix, we explain the map:
\text{Rep}(\text{Tube})(\mathcal{S})\to\text{Rep}(\text{Strip})_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathcal{S})
from the boundary SymTFT
[
13
,
27
,
32
]
. See
[
44
,
46
,
6
]
for details regarding the SymTFT construction.
To describe a system with a boundary, we supply the standard SymTFT with three topological boundary conditions:

•

B_{\text{phys}}
, the dynamical boundary condition encoding the input QFT.

•

B_{\mathcal{S}}
, the first topological boundary condition encoding the symmetry of the theory.

•

B_{\mathcal{M}}
the second topological boundary condition describing the action of symmetry on the boundary.

The three boundaries are connected in the following manner to generate the SymTFT description of a boundary condition for the theory
\mathcal{T}
:

{}{}
{}{}
{}{}

(A.2)

The tube algebra acts linearly on the vector space:

V_{\mu}=\bigoplus_{a}\text{Hom}(\mu,a)\,.

(A.3)

In the presence of the topological boundary
B_{\mathcal{M}}
, the bulk symmetry instead acts, via the Strip algebra, on the space:

W_{\mu}=\bigoplus_{m,n}W_{\mu}^{m,n}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptW_{\mu}^{m,n}=\bigoplus_{a}\text{Hom}(\mu,a)\otimes\text{Hom}(a\otimes m,n)\,.

(A.4)

On the other hand, irreps of the Strip algebra are encoded in the topological lines
K^{v}
confined on
B_{\mathcal{M}}

which act on the vector space:

(A.7)

leading to the OPE of lines

t_{\mathcal{M}}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu}]=\sum_{v}n_{\mu}^{v}K^{v}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptn_{\mu}^{v}=\sum_{x}1\,.

(A.8)

We can now apply this to
B=B_{\mathcal{M}}
to convert a Tube algebra representation
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
into a (reducible) Strip algebra representation on
B_{\mathcal{M}}
: given a vector

|w_{a}^{x,i}\rangle\in W^{\mu}_{m,n}\,,

(A.9)

the bulk to-boundary expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
allows to express it as a linear combination of vectors in
K^{v}_{m,n}
. This shows the claimed map from the perspective of the SymTFT.

Example: Fibonacci

The simplest example is the Fibonacci category Fib. It’s center is
\mathcal{Z}(\text{Fib})=\text{Fib}^{2}
. There is only one topological boundary condition, corresponding to the condensation of the diagonal anyon
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})+(W,W)
. Bulk topological lines
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1}),\,(\mathbbm{1},W),\,(W,\mathbbm{1}),\,(W,W)
have the following expansion:

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})\to\mathbbm{1}\,,

(A.10)

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{1},W)\to W\,,\hskip 20.00003pt(W,\mathbbm{1})\to W\,,

\displaystyle(W,W)\to\mathbbm{1}\oplus W\,.

The Tube algebra thus has three 1-dimensional representations
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1}),\,(\mathbbm{1},W),\,(W,\mathbbm{1})
and one 2-dimensional representation
(W,W)
.
The Strip algebra instead has two irreps,
\mathbbm{1}
, which is two dimensional (both
K^{\mathbbm{1}}_{\mathbbm{1}\mathbbm{1}}
and
K^{\mathbbm{1}}_{WW}
are 1-dimensional), and
W
, which is three dimensional (
K^{W}_{\mathbbm{1}W},\,K^{W}_{W\mathbbm{1}},\,K^{W}_{WW}
are 1-dimensional).

Let us look at the branching:
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})
carries a 1d Tube representation, but it acts on both
\mathbb{H}_{11}
and
\mathbb{H}_{WW}
. Thus it branches in a 2d Strip algebra representation. Similarly,
(\mathbbm{1},W)
has a 1d Tube representation, and branches into the 3d Strip representation by acting on
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}W},\,\mathbb{H}_{W\mathbbm{1}},\,\mathbb{H}_{WW}
. Finally,
(W,W)
carries the 2d Tube representation, and the total space
W_{W}
on which the Strip algebra acts has dimension:

\text{dim}(W_{W})=1\times 2+1\times 3=5\,,

(A.11)

which is compatible only with the branching
W_{W}=K_{\mathbbm{1}}\oplus K_{W}
.

Appendix B
Symmetry-Reflecting Defect via Discrete Gauging

In
[
5
]
we have shown that symmetry reflecting surface defects can be constructed by coupling anomalous (1+1)d matter to the bulk gauge field magnetically.

We describe a similar construction which applies to (1+1)d interfaces. We focus on the case in which the bulk theory has the same anomaly-free
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry both on the left and on the right. We do this starting from a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetric defect, with a nontrivial defect anomaly. After folding, we gauge the full
G=\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry and produce a new
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
symmetry reflecting defect
\widetilde{\calD}
with the nontrivial defect anomaly.

Gauging and folding

We consider the folded system. A defect can be described as a symmetric interface between the dynamical theory
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{L}\times\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{R}
and a symmetric gapped phase
\mathcal{F}
(a TQFT):

B_{\mathcal{F}}
\mathcal{T}
\mathcal{F}

(B.1)

\mathcal{F}
captures the symmetry breaking pattern on the interface. Defect anomalies for
B_{\mathcal{F}}
arise when
\mathcal{F}
contains a nontrivial SPT phase for the relevant symmetry. We leverage this to understand the symmetry realization on
B_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{A}}
after gauging a symmetry
\mathcal{A}
:

\hbox to64.05pt{\vbox to73.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.39688pt\lower-15.59933pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.71246pt}{25.95276pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(B.2)

Internal symmetries

Consider
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and a symmetric defect
\calD
, with nontrivial defect anomaly. We fold the system and gauge
G\times G
.
Before gauging, the defect was described by a TQFT which preserves the diagonal subgroup
G_{\text{diag}}
via a nontrivial SPT
\alpha
, while breaking the quotient:

\mathcal{F}=\text{SPT}_{\alpha}(G_{\text{diag}})\otimes\text{SSB}(G\times G/G_{\text{diag}})\,.

(B.3)

The partition function for the SSB phase is
Z_{SSB}=\delta(A_{L}=A_{R})
, thus we get:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{F}/G\times G}[A_{L},A_{R},B_{L},B_{R}]=\sum_{a,b}\exp\left(i\pi\int ab+a(A_{L}+A_{R})+b(B_{L}+B_{R})\right)

(B.4)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i\pi\int(A_{L}+A_{R})(B_{L}+B_{R})\right)=\exp\left(i\pi\int A_{L}B_{R}+A_{R}B_{L}\right)=\text{SPT}_{\widehat{\alpha}}

\displaystyle.

Thus the defect after gauging preserves
\widehat{G}\times\widehat{G}
(is symmetry reflecting) with the defect anomaly
\widehat{\alpha}
. The computation can be generalized to
G=\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
in the obvious way.

Fermion parity

Another key case is that of a fermionic system, in which the fermion parity symmetry is preserved by the defect with a nontrivial defect anomaly given by the Arf-phase:

\alpha=\text{Arf}\,.

(B.5)

The computation is similar.
\mathcal{F}
breaks
(-)^{F_{L}}\times(-)^{F_{R}}
to the diagonal subgroup, with an Arf SPT.
The bosonization of this system is

Z_{\text{bos}}[B]=\text{Arf}(B\cdot\rho)\text{Arf}(\rho)\sum_{b}Z_{f}[b]e^{i\pi\int bB}\,.

(B.6)

Using that
[
56
]
:

\sum_{b}\text{Arf}(b\cdot\rho)e^{i\pi\int b\cup B}=\text{Arf}(B\cdot\rho)\text{Arf}(\rho)\,,

(B.7)

and

\text{Arf}((A+B)\cdot\rho)=\text{Arf}(A\cdot\rho)\text{Arf}(B\cdot\rho)e^{i\pi\int AB}\,.

(B.8)

We find:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{F}_{bos}}[A_{L},A_{R}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\text{Arf}(a\cdot\rho)e^{i\pi\int a(A_{L}+A_{R})}\text{Arf}(A_{L}\cdot\rho)\text{Arf}(A_{R}\cdot\rho)

(B.9)

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\int A_{L}A_{R}}=\text{SPT}_{\alpha}\,.

Thus bosonizing a fermionic defect with an Arf defect anomaly gives a symmetry reflecting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defect with defect anomaly
\alpha=i\pi\int A_{L}A_{R}
.

Both examples are known from previous studies of non-invertible symmetries:

•

The first case is realized by the duality defect
\mathcal{N}
of Rep
(D_{8})
. This can be obtained by (twisted) gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
symmetric theory, with type III anomaly
i\pi\int A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}
. The
A_{1}
defect has defect anomaly
\alpha=i\pi\int A_{2}A_{3}
and becomes
\mathcal{N}
.

•

In the second case, the chiral fermion parity
(-)^{F_{\chi}}
of the Majorana fermion has a mixed anomaly with
(-)^{F}
. This leads to a defect anomaly for
(-)^{F_{\chi}}
. Upon bosonization the system becomes the Ising CFT, and
(-)^{F_{\chi}}
becomes the KW duality defect
\mathcal{N}
.

These techniques can be applied to many other cases, giving rise to a plethora of symmetry reflecting defects with the wanted defect anomaly.

Appendix C
Details on the Tricritical Ising Flow and the Defect Strip Algebra

In the main text we have analyzed the integrable boundary flow obtained by deforming the tricritical Ising CFT with the relevant deformation
\sigma^{\prime}
. In this appendix we provide some extra details on this analysis.

As well known, among the full set of the topological lines present in the tricritical Ising CFT, a distinguished subset is the one generating the so–called Fibonacci subcategory, also dubbed Fib. The Fibonacci fusion category is a rank-2 category with objects
\{\mathbbm{1},W\}
, fusion rules

W\times W=\mathbbm{1}+W\,,

(C.1)

and one only non-trivial F-symbol

F^{W}_{WWW}=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta^{-1}&\zeta^{-1}\\
1&-\zeta^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(C.2)

where
\zeta=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
is the golden ratio.

To compute various observables, it is usually convenient to employ a diagrammatic calculus in which the object
W
is represented by an unlabeled line, while
\mathbbm{1}
is represented either by the absence of a line or, depending on the context, by a dashed line. In this notation, some of the identities we use are the following:

=
\zeta
=
\zeta
=
-
\zeta^{-1}
=
\zeta^{-1}
+
\zeta^{-1}

(C.3)

Let’s consider how the primary fields organize into representations of the Tube algebra of Fibonacci category. First we recall that the local primaries (i.e. in the untwisted Hilbert space), with their charge under
W
and its holomorphic weights, are

\begin{array}[]{|r|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&1&\varepsilon&\varepsilon^{\prime}&\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}&\sigma&\sigma^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr W:&\zeta&-\zeta^{-1}&-\zeta^{-1}&\zeta&-\zeta^{-1}&\zeta\\
\hline\cr h:&0&\frac{1}{10}&\frac{3}{5}&\frac{3}{2}&\frac{3}{80}&\frac{7}{16}\\
\hline\cr\overline{h}:&0&\frac{1}{10}&\frac{3}{5}&\frac{3}{2}&\frac{3}{80}&\frac{7}{16}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(C.4)

while the content of the
W
twisted sector is

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{{Defect Operator }}&h&\bar{h}&\text{{Spin}}\\
\hline\cr\overline{\psi}&0&\frac{3}{5}&+\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\psi&\frac{3}{5}&0&-\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime}&\frac{3}{5}&\frac{3}{5}&0\\
\hline\cr\tilde{\varepsilon}&\frac{1}{10}&\frac{1}{10}&0\\
\hline\cr\Phi&\frac{1}{10}&\frac{3}{2}&-\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\overline{\Phi}&\frac{3}{2}&\frac{1}{10}&+\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\tilde{\sigma}&\frac{3}{80}&\frac{3}{80}&0\\
\hline\cr\Lambda&\frac{3}{80}&\frac{7}{16}&-\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\overline{\Lambda}&\frac{7}{16}&\frac{3}{80}&+\frac{2}{5}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(C.5)

Fibonacci’s tube algebra.

Since Fib is an MTC, its Drinfeld center is simply the product
\mathcal{Z}(\text{Fib})=\text{Fib}\times\overline{\text{Fib}}
, therefore there are
4
simple anyons

\mathcal{Z}(\text{Fib})=\{(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})\;,\;(\mathbbm{1},W)\;,\;(W,\mathbbm{1})\;,\;(W,W)\}\,,

(C.6)

meaning that the Tube algebra has only
4
irreducible representations. Of these, the three representations
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1}),(\mathbbm{1},W),(W,\mathbbm{1})
are
1
-dimensional, while
(W,W)
is
2
-dimensional.

Now let’s map this to the field content of the Tricritical Ising model. The lagrangian algebra that corresponds to the Fib-symmetric boundary condition (which in this case is also the unique one) is
\mathcal{L}=(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})\oplus(W,W)
, and contains the only two spinless anyons of the Drinfeld center (the spin of
W
is
2/5
in Fib and
-2/5
in
\overline{\text{Fib}}
). This means that the primaries of the untwisted sector transform in either the trivial
(\mathbbm{1},\mathbbm{1})
irrep, or the non-trivial
2
d irrep
(W,W)
. Looking at the eigenvalues of the
W
line on the untwisted sector primaries we see that the identity,
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}
all transform in the trivial irrep, while
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\sigma
transform in the
2
d irrep. This is consistent with the content of the twisted Hilbert space: for all three untwisted primaries
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
\sigma
there is a corresponding twisted sector primary with the same conformal dimensions which completes the doublet. The remaining operators in the
W
-twisted sector transform either in the
(\mathbbm{1},W)
or the
(W,\mathbbm{1})

1
-dimensional irreps depending on the sign of their spin. More formally the Tube algebra is generated by
5
non-trivial elements, the simplest is the
W
line acting on the untwisted Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}}

U_{5}=\hbox to66.24pt{\vbox to12.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.12048pt\lower 22.36229pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Their algebra easily follows from the
F
-symbols. Depending on which representation we want to describe only some of these elements will be non-trivial. Clearly in the trivial irrep only
U_{5}
acts, while in the two non-trivial
1d
irreps only
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
can act. Instead in the
2d
irrep all
5
elements are non-trivial.

The Strip algebra.

The Fibonacci Strip algebra is easy to describe. It is generated by
9
non-trivial elements (on top of those we have the identity in each of the
4
strip Hilbert spaces), these can be loosely divided in two classes. The first acts on strip Hilbert spaces with equal boundary conditions

The algebra again follows from the
F

It is now easy to obtain the explicit representations. For example the in the
2d
irrep only the
3
maps
N_{1},N_{2}
and
N_{3}
are non-trivial and we get

N_{1}|v_{W}\rangle=\zeta^{1/2}|v_{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle\,,\hskip 20.00003ptN_{2}|v_{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=\zeta^{1/2}|v_{W}\rangle\,,\hskip 20.00003ptN_{3}|v_{W}\rangle=\zeta|v_{W}\rangle\,.

(C.12)

One can similarly obtain the explicit maps of the
3d
irrep.

Tubes and Strips.

In this example we can describe more explicitly the relation between Tube and Strip irreps. First we consider the trivial Tube irrep, which acts on
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}}=\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}\mathbbm{1}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{WW}
. At the level of the Strip only
N_{1},N_{2}
and
N_{3}
act within
\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}}
, so the corresponding irrep is the
2d
one. The element
U_{5}
of the Tube is the only one acting non-trivially and its explicit realization in terms of the Strip algebra is (we fix the coefficients imposing the Tube algebra fusion rules)

U_{5}=\frac{1}{\zeta^{1/2}}\left(N_{1}+N_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{\zeta}N_{3}\,.

(C.13)

It acts by

U_{5}|v_{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle=|v_{W}\rangle\,,\hskip 20.00003ptU_{5}|v_{W}\rangle=|v_{\mathbbm{1}}\rangle+|v_{W}\rangle\,,

(C.14)

which is what one expects for the vacua of the theory. Let’s now consider the other Tube irreps. The non-trivial
1d
ones act within
\mathbb{H}_{W}=\mathbb{H}_{W\mathbbm{1}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}W}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{WW}
which also carries the
3d
irrep of the strip algebra. Finally the
2d
Tube irrep acts on

\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{W}=\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}\mathbbm{1}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{WW}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{W\mathbbm{1}}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{\mathbbm{1}W}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{WW}\,,

(C.15)

which carries both the
2d
and
3d
Strip algebra irreps.
